[li id=] M.6 Analysis
[li id=] M.6.1 Benign Task Performance
[li id=] M.6.2 Key Findings
[li id=] Finding 1: In legal practice, harmful execution often precedes refusal, so refusal timing rather than refusal rate alone is the relevant safety metric.
[li id=] Finding 2: Legal domain ambiguity creates attack categories that current models do not reliably refuse, because malicious outputs are structurally indistinguishable from legitimate legal work product.
[li id=] Finding 3: Current models lack robust trust calibration for legal workflow artifacts, so an attacker-planted “PARTNER DIRECTIVE” in a matter note or vault document is treated the same as a genuine one.
[li id=] Finding 4: Under indirect attacks, Claude resists by silently ignoring the injected instruction while finishing the benign task, whereas GPT tends to either fully comply with the injection or abandon the task entirely.
[li id=] Finding 5: Gemini-3-Pro behaves as a one-shot executor that produces the requested document on the first turn with no verification loop, making it the most exposed model in legal.
[li id=] Finding 6: Agent frameworks have asymmetric effects on safety: OpenClaw eliminates partial execution but cannot prevent full compliance with injected instructions.
[li id=] Finding 7: Security and capability are decoupled in the legal domain: Claude-Opus achieves 94% BSR with 9.5–12.5% indirect ASR, while GPT-5.2 achieves 85.7–90.9% BSR with 66% indirect ASR.
[li id=] M.6.3 Red-Teaming Strategy Analysis
[li id=] Indirect: environment injection is uniquely effective in legal.
[li id=] Direct: jailbreaks frame malicious actions as routine legal workflows.

[h1 id=] DecodingTrust-Agent Platform (DTap): A Controllable and Interactive Red-Teaming Platform for AI Agents
[p id=abstract1.1] AI agents are increasingly deployed across diverse domains to automate complex workflows through long-horizon and high-stakes action executions. Due to their high capability and flexibility, such agents raise significant security and safety concerns. A growing number of real-world incidents have shown that adversaries can easily manipulate agents into performing harmful actions, such as leaking API keys, deleting user data, or initiating unauthorized transactions. Evaluating agent security is inherently challenging, as agents operate in dynamic, untrusted environments involving external tools, heterogeneous data sources, and frequent user interactions. However, realistic, controllable, and reproducible environments for large-scale risk assessment remain largely underexplored. To address this gap, we introduce the DecodingTrust-Agent Platform ( DTap ), the first controllable and interactive red-teaming platform for AI agents, spanning 14 real-world domains and over 50 simulation environments that replicate widely used systems such as Google Workspace, Paypal, and Slack. To scale the risk assessment of agents in DTap , we further propose DTap-Red , the first autonomous red-teaming agent that systematically explores diverse injection vectors (e.g., prompt, tool, skill, environment, combinations) and autonomously discovers effective attack strategies tailored to varying malicious goals. Using DTap-Red , we curate DTap-Bench , a large-scale red-teaming dataset comprising high-quality instances across domains, each paired with a verifiable judge following corresponding security policies to automatically validate attack outcomes. Through DTap , we conduct large-scale evaluations of popular AI agents built on various backbone models, spanning security policies, risk categories, and attack strategies, revealing systematic vulnerability patterns and providing valuable insights for developing secure next-generation agents. The platform is open-sourced at https://decodingtrust-agent.com .
[figcaption id=] Figure 1 : DecodingTrust-Agent Platform provides a comprehensive security evaluation for AI agents with advanced red-teaming, covering both indirect and direct threat models.
[h2 id=] 1 Introduction
[figcaption id=] Figure 2 : Overview of DecodingTrust-Agent Platform ( DTap ) . DTap is the first controllable and interactive simulation platform for advanced red-teaming for AI agents. It spans 14 high-stakes domains (e.g., finance, workflows, coding, customer service) and over 50 widely used environments (e.g., Google Workspace, PayPal, Slack), replicating their real-world counterparts while providing realistic agent interfaces. DTap supports diverse injection vectors (prompt, tool, skill, environment, and their combinations) and integrates DTap-Red , an autonomous red-teaming agent that discovers effective attacks using advanced strategies and feedback from verifiable judges.
[p id=S1.p1.1] Autonomous AI agents are rapidly emerging as a foundational paradigm for real-world intelligent systems, with applications spanning personal productivity (e.g., email and calendar management), enterprise workflows (e.g., CRM and finance operations), and complex decision-making pipelines Anthropic (2026a) ; OpenAI (2026) ; OpenClaw (2026b) ; Manus (2026) . By enabling large language models (LLMs) to interact with external environments, invoke tools, and maintain persistent memory, agentic systems significantly extend the capabilities of standalone models and push the frontier of AI. However, this increased autonomy and capabilities also introduce fundamentally new security, safety, and privacy risks, which remain a major barrier to their large-scale deployment, particularly in high-stakes domains such as finance, healthcare, legal services, and critical infrastructure Yang et al. (2024) ; Li et al. (2024) ; Watson et al. (2025) .
[p id=S1.p2.1] To uncover and mitigate these risks, red-teaming, which systematically probes AI systems with diverse adversarial strategies, has become an essential component of AI safety and security evaluation Zou et al. (2023) ; Liu et al. (2024) ; Chen et al. (2025) . While prior work has made progress in red-teaming LLMs, extending these approaches to agentic systems is inherently challenging. Unlike static models, agents operate in dynamic, interactive settings, where they can (i) interact with external environments, (ii) invoke diverse tools with varying semantics, and (iii) leverage specialized harnesses to execute multi-step workflows. Existing agent red-teaming efforts often focus on limited attack surfaces (e.g., direct prompt injection alone) or rely on simplified, static environments, failing to capture the full spectrum of real-world risks Greshake et al. (2023) ; Andriushchenko et al. () ; Debenedetti et al. (2024) ; Zhang et al. () .Here, a key bottleneck lies in the lack of fully controllable and realistic environments that enable systematic, safe, and reproducible evaluation of agent behaviors under adversarial conditions.
[p id=S1.p3.1] In this work, we introduce DecodingTrust-Agent Platform ( DTap ), the first fully controlled, comprehensive real-world simulation platform for advanced red-teaming of agentic systems, as illustrated in Fig. 2 . Our platform enables head-to-head simulation of over 50 realistic environments across 14 domains, including widely used services such as email (e.g., Gmail), scheduling (e.g., Calendar, Zoom), and financial systems (e.g., PayPal, Robinhood). Specifically, DTap replicates the official agent-facing MCPs and GUIs of their real-world counterparts and purpose-builds them for red-teaming, enabling transferable security evaluation while maintaining full control and observability over intermediate states and interactions. This design supports fine-grained manipulation and injection of environment payloads, tool supply chains, and contextual inputs, enabling safe evaluation of adversarial scenarios without real-world consequences. By bridging realism and controllability, DTap provides a scalable foundation for evaluating agent robustness in complex, real-world settings.
[p id=S1.p4.1] Building on this platform, we further propose an autonomous red-teaming agent, DTap-Red , equipped with a range of attack skills and strategies, as well as the ability to manipulate the agent’s execution pipeline. Given a victim agent and malicious goals, DTap-Red systematically explores diverse attack strategies across multiple injection surfaces in an iterative fashion, as illustrated in Fig. 3 . Specifically, DTap-Red exploits victim agents by jointly optimizing both injection payloads and injection locations across prompt-, tool-, skill-, and environment-level attack vectors, as well as their compositional combinations. In addition, DTap-Red leverages verifiable judges that directly inspect outcome states in the environment (e.g., whether an unauthorized transaction has been executed) to determine attack success and provide feedback for unsuccessful attacks to enable iterative improvement. Given the diverse security requirements across applications and regions, we ground our red-teaming in domain-specific risk patterns and derive malicious goals from risk categories systematically extracted from domain policies and regulations (e.g., Salesforce AUP ( Salesforce, Inc., 2023 ) , EU AI Act ( European Union, 2024 ) ) as well as use-case-driven risks. This ensures that each evaluation scenario reflects concrete regulatory requirements and practical risks in enterprise and high-stakes environments.
[p id=S1.p5.1] Given the lack of comprehensive and advanced red-teaming benchmarks for AI agents, we build DTap-Bench , which covers over 4K diverse malicious goals derived from 300+ risk categories extracted from 60+ security policies across 14 domains. In total, DTap-Bench includes 6,682 high-quality tasks, of which 3,876 are red-teaming tasks, each paired with a manually designed verifiable judge to assess task completion or attack success. Specifically, we employ DTap-Red to optimize effective attacks for each malicious goal, followed by human review to ensure their quality and practicality, with the distribution of tasks and injection types shown in Fig. 4 . To evaluate the trade-off between security and utility, DTap-Bench includes both a benign task set for utility evaluation and two separate red-teaming task sets under two threat models: (1) indirect threat model , where a benign user issues a legitimate query while a third-party adversary introduces a hidden malicious goal via indirect injection vectors (e.g., environment injection, MCP tool poisoning, or skill injection); and (2) direct threat model , where the user themselves acts as the attacker and directly issues malicious queries, potentially combined with indirect injections, to induce harmful actions and misuse agents against other users or systems.
[p id=S1.p6.1] We conduct extensive experiments across a wide range of state-of-the-art agentic frameworks on DTap-Bench , including OpenClaw, Claude Code, Google ADK, and OpenAI Agents SDK, with advanced backbone models such as GPT-5.5, Gemini-3-Pro, Claude-Sonnet-4.5, as well as open-source models such as DeepSeek-V4-Pro. As shown in Fig. 1 , all agents exhibit substantial vulnerability under advanced red-teaming attacks. Overall, Google ADK is the most vulnerable under the indirect threat model (55.7% ASR), while OpenClaw (DeepSeek-V4-Pro) exhibits the highest ASR under direct threat model (59.6%). Even the most robust agent, Claude Code, still reaches over 25.2% ASR, indicating that security remains a major challenge for current agentic frameworks and highlighting the effectiveness of DTap-Bench . Our key findings are as follows:
[li id=S1.I1.i1] •
[p id=S1.I1.i1.p1.1] Asymmetric vulnerability across indirect injection surfaces. Skill- and tool-level injections consistently achieve higher ASR than environment injections, suggesting that environment inputs are treated as more external, while internalized channels remain highly exploitable. This asymmetry also varies across frameworks: OpenClaw shows substantially lower ASR on tool injections than OpenAI Agents and Google ADK, indicating its strong trust calibration against external plugins.
[li id=S1.I1.i2] •
[p id=S1.I1.i2.p1.1] Compositional injections are highly effective. Agents are particularly vulnerable to compositional attacks that combine multiple injection vectors, such as email thread fabrication, multi-message contextual injections, multi-step attack chains, and multimodal typographic injections (see Tab. 1 ).
[li id=S1.I1.i3] •
[p id=S1.I1.i3.p1.1] Uneven susceptibility to direct and indirect attacks. OpenAI Agents, Claude Code, and OpenClaw are more vulnerable to direct prompt injections than indirect, indicating strong reliance on user instructions. In contrast, Gemini-based agents show lower ASR under direct attacks, suggesting weaker instruction-following but improved robustness to explicit malicious prompts.
[li id=S1.I1.i4] •
[p id=S1.I1.i4.p1.1] Prompt-level guardrails are insufficient. While agents such as Claude Code and OpenClaw reduce ASR through prompt-level defenses, these protections are limited, and sophisticated attacks can still succeed. Strong security protection requires additional harness-level designs (e.g., external guardrails or by-design execution controls).
[li id=S1.I1.i5] •
[p id=S1.I1.i5.p1.1] Context-aware risks dominate. Agents are more vulnerable to risks that require contextual reasoning and information flow analysis over multi-step action execution (e.g., Data Exfiltration , Sensitive Data Handling , Privilege Escalation ), whereas relatively robust against content-generation risks (e.g., Sexual Content , Weaponization ), potentially due to model-level alignment.
[li id=S1.I1.i6] •
[p id=S1.I1.i6.p1.1] Environment-dependent vulnerability. Communication-centric environments (e.g., Gmail, WhatsApp, Calendar) are significantly more susceptible due to their rich and dynamic interactions, while finance-related environments such as PayPal, E-Commerce, and travel booking exhibit lower ASR, with agents often ignoring even obvious injections in tool descriptions due to their sensitive nature.
[li id=S1.I1.i7] •
[p id=S1.I1.i7.p1.1] Open-source agent backbones are more susceptible to direct misuse. Agents built on open-source models such as DeepSeek-V4-Pro exhibit the highest ASR under the direct threat model, indicating strong instruction-following but weaker ability to distinguish malicious intent. In contrast, closed-source backbones are much more aligned against such direct attacks.
[li id=S1.I1.i8] •
[p id=S1.I1.i8.p1.1] Execute-then-refuse failure mode. OpenAI Agents SDK and Google ADK frequently execute harmful actions before issuing refusals, likely due to batch tool invocation that hampers per-tool consequence reasoning, which significantly contributes to their high ASR.
[figcaption id=] Table 1 : Red-teaming examples with severe consequences from DTap-Bench demonstrating diverse injection vectors and sophisticated attack strategies across environments and risk categories.
[p id=S1.p8.1] In summary, we make the following key contributions:
[li id=S1.I2.i1] •
[p id=S1.I2.i1.p1.1] A comprehensive and fully controllable red-teaming platform, DTap , spanning 50+ simulated, full-stack environments with faithful MCP and GUI replication of their real-world counterparts, purpose-built for agent utility and security evaluation.
[li id=S1.I2.i2] •
[p id=S1.I2.i2.p1.1] An autonomous red-teaming agent, DTap-Red , equipped with 200+ sophisticated attack strategies that systematically explore attacks across diverse injection vectors (prompt, tool, skill, and environment, and their combinations).
[li id=S1.I2.i3] •
[p id=S1.I2.i3.p1.1] A policy-grounded red-teaming benchmark, DTap-Bench , comprising 6,682 tasks and covering 4K+ malicious goals from 300+ risk categories across 14 domains, each paired with effective attacks optimized against a surrogate agent and a verifiable judge that checks environment states.
[li id=S1.I2.i4] •
[p id=S1.I2.i4.p1.1] We invest substantial human and compute resources to build the comprehensive evaluation platform and high-quality benchmark, including over 16,000 expert hours from a team of 17 red-teaming specialists across 20 months, and $120,000 in API credits for data curation and validation.
[li id=S1.I2.i5] •
[p id=S1.I2.i5.p1.1] Extensive evaluations across frontier agent frameworks and models reveal novel and insightful findings regarding their security, offering meaningful guidance for building more secure agents.
[h2 id=] 2 Related Works
[h3 id=] 2.1 Red-Teaming Environments and Simulators for AI Agents
[p id=S2.SS1.p1.1] Prior work on red-teaming environments for AI agents remains limited in both realism and coverage. For example, AgentDojo ( Debenedetti et al., 2024 ) and AgentHarm ( Andriushchenko et al., ) lack dynamic environments and rely on static, synthetic tools with injections embedded in hardcoded outputs, which fail to capture the risks arising from realistic agent dynamics. Similarly, SHADE-Arena ( Kutasov et al., 2025 ) and Petri ( Fronsdal et al., 2025 ) introduce more interactive environments, but these remain highly simplified and cannot faithfully model the diversity of real-world adversaries encountered by agents. On the other hand, ST-4 ( Levy et al., 2025 ) , RedTeamCUA ( Liao et al., 2025 ) , SafeArena ( Tur et al., 2025 ) , and ShieldAgent ( Chen et al., ) aim to evaluate agents in more realistic settings, but are limited to a small number of environments and narrow domains, and often lack key design properties such as flexible state resets and deterministic transitions required for reproducible red-teaming.
[p id=S2.SS1.p2.1] In contrast, DTap includes 50+ full-stack environments purpose-built for red-teaming that replicate their agent interfaces and support high-concurrency evaluation and resetting to arbitrary adversarial states, enabling realistic and efficient simulation with strong transferability to real-world scenarios.
[h3 id=] 2.2 Red-Teaming Benchmarks for AI Agents
[p id=S2.SS2.p1.1] Existing red-teaming benchmarks for AI agents remain limited in their coverage of injection vectors and attack strategies, typically relying on directly appended malicious instructions or naive, heuristic-based prompt injection templates that fail to capture the complex adversarial patterns exhibited by AI agents. For example, AgentDojo ( Debenedetti et al., 2024 ) focuses on unrealistic tool-output injections by directly suffixing outputs with malicious instructions, while AgentHarm ( Andriushchenko et al., ) , ST-Web ( Levy et al., 2025 ) , and SafeArena ( Tur et al., 2025 ) primarily study direct prompt injection without leveraging advanced attack techniques. Other works explore more sophisticated, agent-oriented attacks ( Xu et al., ; Chen et al., 2024 ) , but often assume domain-specific or white-box attacker capabilities, limiting their applicability in realistic threat settings. Moreover, these benchmarks typically lack a systematic risk taxonomy and instead rely on a small set of ad hoc malicious goals, which can lead to biased evaluations and overlook enterprise-critical risk categories.
[p id=S2.SS2.p2.1] In contrast, DTap-Bench is a policy-driven benchmark that covers 4K malicious goals extracted from 60+ real-world security policies and considers a wide array of injection vectors that previous works do not explore, including environment, skill, tool, and prompt, while assuming generic black-box access to the agent. Notably, each instance includes sophisticated attacks end-to-end optimized by DTap-Red from a library of 200+ attack strategies, ensuring both effectiveness and transferability.
[h2 id=] 3 DTap : Unified Platform for Agent Red-Teaming
[p id=S3.p1.1] In this section, we introduce DTap ( Fig. 2 ), a controllable and interactive simulation platform purpose-built for red-teaming AI agents, covering 14 domains and over 50 environments in which agents commonly operate. DTap ’s mission is to provide a realistic, controllable, and dynamic validation layer to rigorously evaluate the security of AI agents under diverse attack vectors and scenarios. We introduce the design principles of DTap in Sec. 3.1 and its domain coverage in Sec. 3.2 .
[figcaption id=] Table 2 : Detailed comparison of DTap with existing agent red-teaming platforms.
[h3 id=] 3.1 Design Principles of DTap
[p id=S3.SS1.p1.1] DTap encompasses 14 diverse domains in which AI agents often perform high-stakes actions, such as handling sensitive data and financial assets. Each domain involves various representative environments, simulated for controllable red-teaming, which closely mirror their real-world interfaces for agent interactions, enabling faithful transfer of security evaluations to real-world settings.
[p id=S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1] Each simulated environment in DTap supports the core functionalities of its real-world counterpart that commonly appear in agent workflows, with industry-grade frontend, backend, and database designs. We replicate the official GUI (e.g., dashboards) to enable realistic interaction for both human users and visual web agents, and align the HTML structure to provide a faithful observation space for agents that rely on textual HTML representations or accessibility trees. We provide a detailed description of each environment in App. P .
[p id=S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.1] Our environments are purpose-built for red-teaming evaluation, designed with several key properties: (1) Dynamic and Stateful Interactions : unlike static benchmarks such as AgentDojo ( Debenedetti et al., 2024 ) that fail to capture real-world risk dynamics, DTap enables fully dynamic, multi-turn agent explorations in the environments while preserving intermediate states for verifiable evaluation; (2) Reproducible Evaluation : DTap provides deterministic environment transitions that allow consistent replay and repeatable risk analysis of agent trajectories; (3) Flexible Reset by Design : all environments provide efficient reset endpoints to instantiate into arbitrary state snapshots, enabling rapid reproduction of target attack scenarios; and (4) Fully Parallelizable : all environments are containerized and parallels through multi-tenant isolation to enable scalable evaluation with low compute overhead.
[p id=S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p3.1] With these controllable environments, it is possible to configure them with diverse injection points that accept external, potentially compromised payloads. For example, in Gmail , benign users may receive emails containing sophisticated prompt injections that aim to mislead the agent, while Google Calendar and Zoom may receive external invitations embedded with malicious instructions. Similarly, websites such as eBay and travel booking platforms may be poisoned through injections in public reviews or comments. Specifically, our red-teaming agent DTap-Red has seamlessly integrated these environment injection points into its harness framework, enabling fully autonomous attack optimization, as elaborated in Sec. 4 .
[p id=S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1] To evaluate tool-use agents in DTap , we construct realistic MCP-based tool interfaces for each environment. These MCPs are replicas of their official counterparts, or widely used community implementations when official versions are unavailable. We ensure one-to-one mappings of tool specifications, including tool names, descriptions, input schema, and return formats. This will ensure the faithful transferability of evaluation results. We provide a taxonomy of tools for each MCP server in App. P .
[p id=S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p2.1] Moreover, real-world MCPs operate on live systems, where tool calls can lead to irreversible and potentially harmful consequences. In contrast, our MCPs are connected to the aforementioned sandboxed environments, where all actions are fully controlled and reversible, providing a safe and controlled setting for red-teaming agents prior to deployment.
[p id=S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1.1] As illustrated in Fig. 3 , DTap bridges agents and diverse environments through a unified MCP interface, enabling seamless integration of tool-use agents. In practice, any agent that supports MCP can be readily incorporated into DTap in a plug-and-play manner.
[p id=S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p2.1] DTap provides built-in support for a range of popular agent frameworks, including OpenAI Agents SDK ( OpenAI, 2025b ) , Google ADK ( Google, 2025 ) , and LangChain Agents ( LangChain, 2025 ) , as well as coding agents such as Claude Code ( Anthropic, 2025 ) and computer-use agents such as OpenClaw ( OpenClaw, 2026a ) . In addition, other agents, including browser-based agents (e.g., ChatGPT-Atlas ( OpenAI, 2025a ) ), UI-driven computer-use agents (e.g., Claude Cowork ( Anthropic, 2026b ) ), and IDE-based coding agents (e.g., Cursor ( Anysphere, 2023 ) ), can also be integrated, as they expose standard MCP-compatible interfaces.
[h3 id=] 3.2 Comprehensive Domains and Environments on DTap Platform
[p id=S3.SS2.p1.1] DTap covers a diverse set of domains, including Workflow , Customer Relationship Management (CRM) , Customer Service , Travel , Code , Browser , Research , OS-Filesystem , Windows , macOS , Finance , Legal , Telecommunication Service (Telecom) , and Medical Service . These domains span major agent workflows involving complex dynamics and high-stakes actions under real-world regulatory and operational constraints, necessitating rigorous evaluation of potential security risks. To support domain-specific security needs, DTap is designed to be policy-following : for each domain, it collects relevant policies, regulations, and use-case–driven risks, and systematically derives risk categories to guide the subsequent red-teaming. Details for each domain are provided in App. B to App. O .
[p id=S3.SS2.p2.1] For each domain, we construct the evaluation with a diverse set of representative environments, covering widely used real-world platforms such as Gmail , Google Calendar , Google Docs , Google Sheets , Google Drive , Google Forms , Slack , PayPal , Zoom , Atlassian , WhatsApp , Telegram , Salesforce CRM , Windows , macOS , Filesystem , ServiceNow , Browser , E-Bay , Booking.com , Expedia , United Airlines , Southwest Airlines , Enterprise , arXiv , Yahoo Finance , Robinhood , Telecom Services , Github , Snowflake , Databricks , X , Reddit , and DoorDash , totaling over 50 environments. Each domain may require agents to interact with multiple relevant environments across diverse use cases, thus introducing sophisticated attack dynamics within these interaction flows.
[p id=S3.SS2.p3.1] We have provided a detailed introduction to each environment in App. P , where for each environment, we provide details on how the GUI and MCP interface are constructed to be realistic according to their real-world counterpart, including screenshots for each environment and a table of corresponding MCP tool list, grouped by their functionalities for each environment.
[h2 id=] 4 DTap-Red : Autonomous Red-Teaming Agent for Agentic Systems
[p id=S4.p1.1] Given the high dimension of agentic attack vectors, we design DTap-Red , an advanced, fully autonomous, and policy-following red-teaming agent that optimizes for effective attacks against arbitrary AI agents to achieve given malicious goals. We first describe the end-to-end red-teaming procedure of DTap-Red in Sec. 4.1 , and then introduce the harness framework design in Sec. 4.2 , including novel components such as an attack algorithm skill library , a spatiotemporal action space over diverse injection types , a multi-layer memory module, and iterative optimization with verifiable judge feedback.
[h3 id=] 4.1 Overview of DTap-Red
[p id=S4.SS1.p1.1] As illustrated in Fig. 3 , DTap-Red operates in a closed-loop, end-to-end pipeline with three stages: (i) attack optimization and generation, (ii) attack execution against the victim agent, and (iii) verifiable judge evaluation with iterative refinement. Specifically, DTap-Red requires only malicious goals as input with corresponding verifiable judges that determine whether the goal has been achieved in the environments, and can fully automate the attack injection optimization process.
[p id=S4.SS1.p2.1] Given a malicious goal as input, DTap-Red first retrieves past experiences from a multi-layer memory module, which contains insights and trajectories from both successful attacks and common failure modes for similar goals. It then invokes relevant attack skills from a diverse skill library, including attack algorithms (e.g., jailbreaks such as flip-attack and base64 encoding) and injection strategies (e.g., optimized payloads injected into tools, skills, environments, and their combinations). Informed by these contexts, DTap-Red performs diverse injections across user prompts, tool descriptions, agent skills, and environment payload sources to induce the victim agent to achieve the malicious goal. Notably, DTap-Red can sequentially compose injections across multiple steps, forming sophisticated exploit chains that collectively compromise the victim agent in a stealthy manner.
[p id=S4.SS1.p3.1] After each attack turn, the verifiable judge will evaluate the attack success by checking environment states. If the attack fails, an attack refinement judge analyzes the injection sequence and the victim agent’s trajectory to provide feedback for subsequent iterations (e.g., whether the injection was not observed or was explicitly rejected). Based on this feedback, DTap-Red iteratively refines its injection strategies to improve effectiveness and ultimately achieve the malicious goal.
[figcaption id=] Figure 3 : Overview of red-teaming agent DTap-Red . Given a malicious goal, DTap-Red first retrieves past experiences from a multi-layer memory module and invokes relevant attack algorithms and injection strategies from its attack skill library. It then performs diverse injections to induce the victim agent to achieve the malicious goal, where the optimized injection can be delivered via user prompt, MCP tools, agent skills, or other manipulable injection points in external environments. After each attack turn, a verifiable judge evaluates whether the attack succeeds; if it fails, an attack refinement judge provides feedback to the red-teaming agent for iterative optimization until success or budget exhaustion. The generated high-quality attacks are collected in DTap-Bench .
[h3 id=] 4.2 Red-Teaming Agentic Framework: DTap-Red
[p id=S4.SS2.p1.1] We describe the key components and mechanisms underlying the effectiveness of DTap below.
[p id=S4.SS2.p2.1] Policy-Following Attack Generation. DTap-Red is designed to be fully policy-following, generating attacks for policy-adherent malicious goals to ensure that risk assessment aligns with specific security requirements of different platforms. It first extracts risk categories from domain-specific policies (e.g., Google Platform AUP for Gmail ), regulations (e.g., FINRA for finance), or use-case–driven risks, and then derives severe malicious goals for each category. Given a malicious goal, DTap-Red strictly follows it to induce the victim agent to perform corresponding malicious actions, and evaluates success using a verifiable judge to ensure that the goal is genuinely achieved.
[p id=S4.SS2.p3.1] Diverse Attack Skill Library. To facilitate the generation of advanced and diverse attacks, we equip DTap-Red with a comprehensive skill library comprising two complementary components:
[p id=S4.SS2.p4.1] (1) Attack algorithms , which include jailbreak and prompt injection methods that transform malicious instructions into more stealthy formats (e.g., GCG ( Zou et al., 2023 ) , ArtPrompt ( Jiang et al., 2024 ) ). These algorithms can be applied across multiple injection points, including direct user prompts and indirect channels such as tools, skills, and environment payloads. Specifically, DTap-Red is equipped with a controllable environment to execute these algorithm scripts for on-the-fly optimization of injection payloads.
[p id=S4.SS2.p5.1] (2) Injection strategies , which consist of knowledge for crafting effective injection content and selecting appropriate injection points (e.g., prompts, tools, environments, and agent skills) to maximize impact. For example, an attacker may construct a fabricated conversation thread between a user and a third party to create a false sense of agreement around a malicious action; such content is particularly effective when injected via realistic communication channels, such as a Gmail email or messages from WhatsApp or Telegram . Similarly, phishing links are more effective when injected into reviews or comments on public websites (e.g., travel booking or e-Bay), redirecting users under the pretense of providing additional information.
[p id=S4.SS2.p6.1] Flexible Injection Action Space. DTap-Red includes spatially diverse injection points in its action space that are commonly exploited in agent workflows, including user prompts, tool descriptions, skill metadata and bodies, and diverse environment data sources. DTap-Red can inject them flexibly via suffixes, prefixes, or full rewrites, depending on the configurable constraints of threat model and attacker capability. In addition, DTap-Red can compose these injection actions temporally across multiple steps to produce stealthy and effective attacks (e.g., individually benign injections that become malicious in combination). To further harness environment injection, we equip each environment with an injection MCP server, where each tool corresponds to a pre-configured injection entry point and payloads are passed via tool parameters. These servers expose controllable and practical injection interfaces that can be manipulated in real-world settings across these environments, enabling DTap-Red to optimize attacks under realistic constraints.
[p id=S4.SS2.p7.1] Multi-Layer Memory Module. DTap-Red is equipped with an advanced memory module that stores both insights and trajectories from successful attacks as well as common failure modes. By design, DTap-Red organizes red-teaming experiences into three layers to capture key dimensions, including risk categories , malicious goals , and threat models with varying injection capabilities. The memory module serves as a core component in the lifelong learning process of DTap-Red , improving both attack effectiveness and optimization efficiency. To balance exploration and exploitation, DTap-Red adopts an -greedy policy ( Chen et al., 2025 ) , where in early stages, the agent fully explores the attack space to accumulate diverse experiences, while in later stages, it exploits them to improve efficiency.
[p id=S4.SS2.p8.1] Iterative Optimization with Verifiable Judge Feedback. After each attack execution against the victim agent, DTap-Red invokes the provided verifiable judge that directly checks the resulting environment states to rigorously assess whether the malicious goal has been achieved. Rather than relying on agent trajectories or LLM-based judges, which are susceptible to reward hacking as seen in prior works (e.g., ( Andriushchenko et al., ; Debenedetti et al., 2024 ) ), the verifiable judge reduces false positives by evaluating concrete outcomes in the environment. For example, it verifies whether the target sensitive data has been exfiltrated to the attacker’s account or whether an unverified transaction has been processed in PayPal .
[p id=S4.SS2.p9.1] If an attack fails, DTap-Red invokes a refinement judge to analyze both the attack and the victim agent’s trajectory, providing feedback on failure modes and potential improvements. For instance, if an injected email is not observed by the agent, DTap-Red may attempt alternative injection channels (e.g., tool descriptions) to increase observability. If an injection is explicitly rejected, DTap-Red can apply more advanced jailbreak algorithms to improve stealthiness. DTap-Red iteratively refines its attacks with such judge feedback until success or the optimization budget is exhausted.
[h2 id=] 5 DTap-Bench : Benchmarking AI Agents with Advanced Red-Teaming
[p id=S5.p1.1] Based on DTap , we collect high-quality agent red-teaming trajectories generated by DTap-Red across diverse domains to construct DTap-Bench benchmark. Specifically, DTap-Bench comprises benign and successful attack agentic trajectories for a wide range of malicious goals under comprehensive risk categories, which are derived from domain-specific policies, regulations, and use-case–driven risks. We first describe the dataset structure and present some key statistics, followed by a detailed overview of the red-teaming data construction pipeline in Sec. 5.1 .
[figcaption id=] Figure 4 : Dataset statistics of DTap-Bench . Left: Distribution of all tasks by domain; Center: Distribution of attack injection types (or combinations) across red-teaming tasks; Right: Mean and confidence interval of the minimum number of tool calls across tasks by domain.
[h3 id=] 5.1 Benchmark Overview
[p id=S5.SS1.p1.1] Dataset Structure. DTap-Bench provides over 6k high-quality scenarios for AI agents across 14 domains and over 50 environments, comprising three subsets: (1) Benign task set , which includes diverse, carefully designed tasks involving high-stakes actions to evaluate agents’ utility in handling critical real-world workflows; (2) Indirect red-teaming task set , which pairs benign tasks with malicious goals under the indirect threat model; (3) Direct red-teaming task set , which specifies explicit misuse goals under the direct threat model. We present key statistics of DTap-Bench in Fig. 4 and defer additional details to Sec. A.1 .
[figcaption id=] Figure 5 : TSNE of the agent trajectories (Claude Code) of DTap and AgentDojo.
[p id=S5.SS1.p2.1] Task. Each task in DTap-Bench includes: (1) a config file defining the task instruction, agent setup (e.g., system prompt and required MCPs), injection sequence for reproducing red-teaming attacks, ensuring deterministic evaluation; (2) setup scripts that seed user data in the corresponding environments (e.g., personalized emails in a user’s inbox) to establish realistic initial conditions; (3) concrete, verifiable judge that directly evaluates whether the malicious goal has been achieved based on the resulting environment states. See Sec. A.3 for details.
[p id=S5.SS1.p3.1] Red-Teaming Task Set. For all red-teaming tasks, DTap-Bench includes attack injection sequences optimized by DTap-Red under a maximum budget of 10 iterations that successfully compromise a surrogate agent (e.g., OpenAI Agents SDK with GPT-5.1) under the constraints of each threat model. These deterministic attack sequences enable fair comparison across agents by evaluating them on a shared set of zero-shot transferable attacks. Specifically, we manually select high-quality attacks across injection types (e.g., prompt, tool, skill, environment, and combinations) to ensure a balanced and diverse coverage.
[p id=S5.SS1.p4.1] Policy-Driven Data Construction Pipeline. All red-teaming scenarios in DTap-Bench are policy-aligned. Red-teaming experts first collect domain-specific policies and use-case–driven risks, and then extract severe risk categories. Based on them, we manually design diverse malicious goals and verifiable judges that evaluate whether the goals are achieved in the environment. For seed data that sets tasks’ initial conditions, we propose a persona-based synthesis pipeline that samples diverse user attributes to construct various profiles, and then generates corresponding benign data consistent with these profiles, ensuring realism and diversity (e.g., personalized email threads in a user’s account).
[p id=S5.SS1.p5.1] Cost and Human Effort. To ensure the high-quality of DTap-Bench , this project devotes substantial human effort and API resources to validate the agent trajectories. In total, it involved approximately 16,000 human hours from a team of 17 red-teaming experts over 20 months, along with approximately $120,000 USD in API credits for attack generation and data curation. These investments enable DTap to serve as a valuable platform for in-depth evaluation and understanding of the security and safety landscape of AI agents.
[h2 id=] 6 Experiments
[h3 id=] 6.1 Experiment Settings
[p id=S6.SS1.p1.1] Agent Frameworks. We evaluate four widely used agent frameworks: OpenAI Agents SDK, Claude Code, Google ADK, and OpenClaw. Specifically, for the OpenAI Agents SDK, we evaluate GPT-5.4, GPT-5.2, and GPT-OSS-120B as backbone models; for OpenClaw, we evaluate GPT-5.5, GPT-5.2, and DeepSeek-V4-Pro. In addition, we evaluate Claude Code with Sonnet-4.5 and Google ADK with Gemini-3-Pro. This enables framework- and model-level comparisons across agent configurations.
[p id=S6.SS1.p2.1] Evaluation Process. We evaluate all six agent configurations on DTap-Bench with high-quality attacks optimized by DTap-Red across 14 domains, including 1,998 indirect red-teaming tasks and 1,878 direct red-teaming tasks to assess robustness under attack, as well as 2,806 benign tasks to measure utility in the absence of attacks, as described in Sec. 5.1 .
[p id=S6.SS1.p3.1] Metrics. (1) Average Attack Success Rate (ASR): An attack is considered successful if the malicious goal is fully achieved. We report ASR separately for each threat model on red-teaming tasks when the agent is under attack; (2) Benign Task Success Rate (BSR): A benign task is considered successful if the instruction is fully completed. We report BSR for benign tasks without applying any attacks.
[h3 id=] 6.2 Main Results
[p id=S6.SS2.p1.1] We present overall evaluation results across domains for both threat models with ASR reported in Tab. 3 , and benign utility measured by BSR in Tab. 4 . We also provide detailed results across diverse risk categories for each domain, including Workflow App. B , CRM App. C , Customer Service App. D , Travel App. E , Code App. F , Browser App. G , Research App. H , OS-Filesystem App. I , Windows App. J , macOS App. K , Finance App. L , Legal App. M , Telecom App. N , and Medical Service App. O .
[p id=S6.SS2.p2.1] We validate the severity of the malicious goals by directly providing them as inputs to the agents without applying any optimized attacks. Across domains (e.g., Tab. 10 in App. B and Tab. 15 in App. C ), almost all agents refuse to follow these instructions, resulting in near-zero ASR, which indicates that the malicious goals are inherently severe and agents naturally reject them due to their consequences. However, when applying strategically attacks optimized by DTap (against GPT-5.1), the ASR increases substantially across domains (see Tab. 3 ) and across risk categories within each domain (e.g., Tab. 11 and Tab. 16 ), demonstrating the effectiveness and transferability of these attacks.
[p id=S6.SS2.p3.1] As shown in Tab. 3 and 4 , robustness varies significantly across the eight agents and is mostly uncorrelated with their utility. Among capable agents (BSR 75%), Google ADK (Gemini-3-Pro) is most vulnerable to indirect attacks (55.7% ASR) while achieving the highest utility (87.0% BSR), whereas OpenClaw (DeepSeek-V4-Pro) and OpenAI Agents SDK (GPT-5.2) are the most vulnerable to direct attacks (59.6% and 58.8% ASR, respectively). In contrast, Claude Code (Sonnet-4.5) is most robust under both threat models (25.2% indirect, 26.9% direct ASR) while maintaining strong utility (80.8% BSR). However, OpenAI Agents SDK (GPT-OSS-120B) is neither secure nor capable, complying with most attacks yet often fails to fully complete the malicious goals due to poor performance, achieving a low BSR of 36.2% and relatively high ASR (46.5% direct, 28.5% indirect).
[figcaption id=] Table 3 : Attack success rate (ASR) (%) across two threat models: (1) Indirect , where a third-party attacker introduces malicious instructions indirectly to manipulate the agent during a benign user task; and (2) Direct , where the attacker directly acts as the user and attempts to manipulate the agent to achieve malicious goals. We evaluate multiple agent frameworks and models across domains. The highest ASR in each column is in bold , indicating the most vulnerable agent configuration.
[figcaption id=] Table 4 : Benign task success rate (BSR) (%) across multiple agent frameworks and models over diverse domains. Higher BSR indicates better capability in successfully completing benign user tasks. The best capability performance in each column is in bold .
[h3 id=] 6.3 Analysis
[p id=S6.SS3.p1.1] Agents exhibit significant asymmetries across threat models. OpenAI Agents SDK, Claude Code, and OpenClaw consistently show higher robustness to indirect injections than to direct prompt attacks (with over a 20% gap), suggesting that these agents can effectively resist common indirect injection patterns (e.g., fabricated authority or urgency framing), while treating user prompts as more trusted and prioritizing instruction-following. In contrast, Google ADK exhibits a lower ASR under direct attacks (47.9%) than under indirect attacks (55.7%), indicating strong prompt-level guardrails that filter malicious requests but are less effective against adversarial content embedded in external inputs.
[p id=S6.SS3.p2.1] Injection susceptibility is asymmetric and amplified by composition. As shown in Tab. 5 , agents exhibit uneven vulnerability across indirect injection surfaces, with skill- and tool-level injections consistently achieving higher ASR than environment injections (e.g., 83.3% vs. 22.2% in GPT-5.4), suggesting that environment inputs are treated as more external while internalized channels remain highly exploitable. This asymmetry also varies across frameworks, where OpenClaw shows substantially lower ASR on tool injections (21.1%) compared to Google ADK (63.4%), indicating stronger safeguards against external plugins. Notably, these vulnerabilities are further amplified under compositional attacks: combining injection vectors (e.g., skill+tool , env+tool ) often increases ASR beyond individual channels (e.g., 50.1% 65.6% in GPT-5.2), demonstrating that multi-surface attacks can more effectively manipulate agent behavior.
[p id=S6.SS3.p3.1] Harness engineering is one key bottleneck in the agentic capability–security tradeoff. While model-level safety alignment contributes to robustness (e.g., GPT-5.4 reduces indirect ASR by 6.7% compared to GPT-5.2), Figure 1 shows that, for the majority of evaluated agents, harness design plays a dominant role in determining robustness. For example, using GPT-5.2 as the backbone, replacing the OpenAI Agents SDK harness with OpenClaw reduces direct ASR from 58.8% to 38.6% and indirect ASR from 46.7% to 35.6%, yielding a combined % reduction in ASR at a minimal utility cost (2%). This suggests that more secure harness designs (e.g., tool call sandboxing, pre-execution consequence reasoning, and trusted-input isolation) can substantially improve the security margin of agentic tasks at low cost, without relying solely on expensive model-level alignment.
[figcaption id=] Table 5 : Attack success rate (ASR, %) across individual injection types and their combinations across all red-teaming tasks. The highest ASR is in bold , indicating the most vulnerable agent configuration.
[p id=S6.SS3.p4.1] Agents are generally more vulnerable to context-aware, distributed risks and more robust to content-level risks. Across risk categories in each domain, the highest ASRs concentrate in context-aware categories, while the lowest appear in content-level ones. For example, in the CRM domain ( Fig. 6 ), agents are highly vulnerable to categories such as Data Exfiltration and Dangerous Actions , where harm arises through multi-step tool sequences that resemble legitimate operations, and most robust to categories such as General AI Restrictions and Fraud / Impersonation , where harm reduces to generating overtly disallowed outputs. This suggests that, for content-level risks, agents can rely on backbone model alignment to refuse harmful outputs, whereas context-aware risks, which requires intensive information flow analysis and reasoning over multi-step tool interactions, remain largely unaddressed.
[p id=S6.SS3.p5.1] Agents with open-source backbones are more susceptible to direct misuse. OpenClaw with open-source backbones such as DeepSeek-V4-Pro exhibits strong instruction-following (83.3% BSR), often regardless of underlying malicious intent, which translates into high direct ASR (59.6%). In contrast, closed-source models are better aligned to resist such misuse and demonstrate stronger robustness to direct attacks (e.g., GPT-5.2 with 38.6% and GPT-5.5 with 28.9% direct ASR).
[p id=S6.SS3.p6.1] An execute-then-refuse failure mode dominates OpenAI Agents SDK and Google ADK. We frequently observe ( App. L , C and B ) that OpenAI Agents SDK with GPT backbones and Google ADK (Gemini-3-Pro) tend to execute harmful tool calls first and refuse afterwards , as illustrated in Fig. 28 in Sec. C.6.2 . We conjecture that this behavior stems from their harness designs that prioritize batch tool invocation, which may hinder reasoning and intervention over intermediate tool-call consequences. In contrast, Claude Code and OpenClaw enforce sequential tool invocation, enabling more explicit control over risks arising in multi-step executions.
[figcaption id=] Figure 6 : Per-category result in the CRM domain. Left: BSR across different task categories; Center: ASR across different risk categories under the indirect threat model; Right: ASR across different risk categories under the direct threat model. Results of other domains are in App. B to App. O
[h2 id=] 7 Conclusions
[p id=S7.p1.1] We introduce DecodingTrust-Agent Platform ( DTap ), the first fully controllable platform for comprehensive red-teaming of AI agents, together with DTap-Red , an autonomous red-teaming agent, and DTap-Bench , a large-scale, policy-grounded benchmark. Through realistic environment simulations and large-scale evaluations, we uncover systematic vulnerability patterns in state-of-the-art agents, including susceptibility to compositional attacks and harness-level failure modes. These results show that existing safeguards are insufficient for real-world deployment and highlight the need for holistic, environment-aware security designs. Overall, DTap provides a scalable foundation for rigorous agent evaluation and advances the development of more secure and reliable agentic systems.
